The daily field guide for digital media

AI Media Scout

2026-11-11 · All platforms · Open-source software

Full expanded open-source creative-AI discovery across 98 observed fields, 836 repository queries and 24 model tasks, supplemented by live primary-source research outside GitHub. This edition provides 44 detailed guides and 47 additional eligible screened discoveries. Six guides complete earlier screened entries, and OpenFilm has a concrete 0.3.2 release update; other first guides are baselines, not claims that those tools launched today. Coverage includes local audio/video, code-assisted films, neural/agent 3D and XR, typography, CAD/fabrication, pose-driven performance, game AI and a GitLab-hosted MLX video editor. Software permissions are separated from model, asset, host and paid-service terms; restricted or unproven workflows remain excluded or pending. Every included entry has a dated six-check assessment. Documentation and published examples were reviewed; no discovered app was installed or run, and no new entry is presented as independently recommended. The existing 2,400-entry weekly review was already complete this week; new entries are checked now and final whole-library coverage is verified after export. Field/source-group findings and failed or size-bounded source captures are disclosed; no exhaustive census is claimed. Verified collection: 836/836 repository queries attempted; 836 successful, 0 partial, 0 failed and 96 bounded/truncated. All 24 model tasks were attempted; 0 failed. These are bounded discovery attempts, not an exhaustive census or quality approval. Empty model-source responses: text2text-generation. An empty response does not establish that these creative workflows have no tools. Separate from successful search queries, 43 watchlist fetches and 3 enrichment attempts failed; the detailed warnings remain in the report. A 404/409 is an availability gap, not proof of abandonment or license revocation.

Search and review counts

98 creative fields; 836/836 repository queries attempted; 24/24 model-task queries attempted. 45233 distinct source candidates and 1022 model leads. 44 detailed profiles and 47 additional screened discoveries. Source gaps: 0 failed repository queries, 0 partial repository queries, 96 bounded repository queries, 0 failed model-task queries and 0 web ecosystem gaps. Raw search candidates include duplicates of known tools, excluded projects and projects awaiting review; they are not verified recommendations.

Profiles are based on current primary documentation. Creative quality and performance claims are attributed to the developers unless hands-on testing is explicitly recorded. Unknown hardware requirements stay unknown. Code, model weights, hosted services, and required proprietary hosts can have different terms.

How it uses AI

A fabrication-specific pipeline asks an image model to redraw an illustration, uses Claude vision to score fidelity and connected outlines, bridges isolated regions and traces the chosen raster into SVG. [1] [3]

Introduction

A fabrication-specific pipeline asks an image model to redraw an illustration, uses Claude vision to score fidelity and connected outlines, bridges isolated regions and traces the chosen raster into SVG. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Preparing stained-glass, silhouette and laser-cut concept patterns from a clear illustration, with a visible redraw/review loop. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The README and project site show source, AI redraw, vector and cut examples. They are developer demonstrations; neither those examples nor a connected SVG certify your material, kerf or minimum feature size. [1] [3]

Install

Clone the official source, use Python 3.10+ and install the editable package. Install Potrace separately; librsvg is optional for previews. Configure OpenAI and Anthropic access locally; an existing supported Claude CLI plan can replace the review API. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Clone the official source, use Python 3.10+ and install the editable package. Install Potrace separately; librsvg is optional for previews. Configure OpenAI and Anthropic access locally; an existing supported Claude CLI plan can replace the review API.
git clone https://github.com/rainesdrew/kerfline.git
cd kerfline
pip install -e .
kerfline my-image.png --output-dir output/

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Choose an illustration with clear boundaries and configure provider access in your local environment.
  2. Run a small redraw; inspect the selected redraw, bridges and the summary verdict rather than trusting the AI score alone.
  3. Compare both VTracer and Potrace SVGs; check minimum features, scale and kerf in your fabrication software before cutting.

macOS and Linux dependency examples are documented; a Windows installation path is not documented.

  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. The software grant is Apache-2.0. GPT-image and Claude are separately operated, proprietary model services; no local open-weight generation route is documented.

How it uses AI

A desktop/local-web media workspace combines downloading with genuine local Whisper speech recognition and exports transcripts, timestamped notes and subtitles. Caption retrieval alone is a conventional fallback, not an AI result. [1] [3]

Introduction

A desktop/local-web media workspace combines downloading with genuine local Whisper speech recognition and exports transcripts, timestamped notes and subtitles. Caption retrieval alone is a conventional fallback, not an AI result. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Transcribing your recorded interviews and reference clips for editing, finding quoted passages and preparing SRT/VTT files for a creative project. [1] [3]

BrickGPT

Scope: Exploring brick sculpture and small architectural concepts from text, while retaining a brick-by-brick representation for inspection.

Evidence checked 2026-11-11; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Reproducible setup · documented: Obtain the official repository, uv and access to the gated Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct base. Run uv sync. The infer/render route additionally requires the ImportLDraw submodule, the separately downloaded background image and LDraw parts. Physics-based checking needs Gurobi and its license; the documented connectivity fallback is less accurate. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.

The gated Meta base retains its own Llama terms; ImportLDraw, FlashTex, LDraw data and Gurobi retain separate grants. Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate. [1] [3] [4]

How it uses AI

A language model fine-tuned for text-to-brick generation proposes brick placements, with stability checking and rollback options; the repository also supplies separate rendering, texturing and mesh-to-brick modules. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A language model fine-tuned for text-to-brick generation proposes brick placements, with stability checking and rollback options; the repository also supplies separate rendering, texturing and mesh-to-brick modules. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Exploring brick sculpture and small architectural concepts from text, while retaining a brick-by-brick representation for inspection. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The official paper site and README show generated structures, colored variants and human assemblies. Those results apply to the authors’ evaluated builds; they do not certify an arbitrary new sculpture. [1] [3] [4]

Obtain the official repository, uv and access to the gated Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct base. Run uv sync. The infer/render route additionally requires the ImportLDraw submodule, the separately downloaded background image and LDraw parts. Physics-based checking needs Gurobi and its license; the documented connectivity fallback is less accurate. [1] [3] [4]

First project

  3. Check part availability and assembly, then validate a small real build before scaling the design. Treat texture and mesh conversion as separate workflows with their own setup.

How it uses AI

A coding-agent framework builds brick sculptures through a Python SDK, validates real-part placement, checks collisions and floating/disconnected elements, and exports editable LDraw models. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Introduction

A coding-agent framework builds brick sculptures through a Python SDK, validates real-part placement, checks collisions and floating/disconnected elements, and exports editable LDraw models. [1] [3] [4] [5]

What it is good for

Iterative text- or image-guided brick concept design with a visible generate, render, validate and revise loop. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Demo & examples

The author project page includes orbitable designs and downloadable LDraw examples. Its reported validity rates are scoped author benchmark results; they are not physical strength or assembly certification for your prompt. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Install

Install the BrickWright CLI with uv, or obtain the source. Connect an external coding agent or configure an API provider locally. Download the LDraw parts library and set LDRAW_LIBRARY_PATH if you need previews; rendering is disabled without it. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Install the BrickWright CLI with uv, or obtain the source. Connect an external coding agent or configure an API provider locally. Download the LDraw parts library and set LDRAW_LIBRARY_PATH if you need previews; rendering is disabled without it.
uv tool install brickwright
brickwright generate "a detailed cruise ship"
brickwright generate --help

  3. Export LDraw, inspect the bill of parts and check actual assembly constraints independently.

How it uses AI

An MCP server gives an LLM semantic brick placement, catalog, preview, validation and LDraw export tools, instead of making it manipulate a CAD interface through clicks. [1]

Introduction

An MCP server gives an LLM semantic brick placement, catalog, preview, validation and LDraw export tools, instead of making it manipulate a CAD interface through clicks. [1]

What it is good for

Agent-guided stud-connected architectural brick concepts, editable MPD files, part lists and step-by-step previews. [1]

Demo & examples

The README shows a generated fortress, a viewer session and imported model renders. The author explicitly says the design task remains hard and the connection model is stud-only. [1]

Install

Install the official Python tool with uv, download the LDraw library with lego-mcp install-library, and register the executable in your MCP client. Claude Desktop is the documented inline-preview route; the standalone agent needs separate Anthropic access. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Install the official Python tool with uv, download the LDraw library with lego-mcp install-library, and register the executable in your MCP client. Claude Desktop is the documented inline-preview route; the standalone agent needs separate Anthropic access.
git clone https://github.com/jonx/BrickBuilderMCP.git
cd BrickBuilderMCP
uv tool install --from . lego-mcp
lego-mcp install-library

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Connect the server in your MCP client and start a very small stud-aligned model.
  2. Prefer the documented valid-placement and high-level wall/room tools; inspect connectivity, overlap and structural reports after revisions.
  3. Save a project, export MPD/LDraw and inspect the part list before trying a physical assembly.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
The author documents roughly 150 MB for the LDraw library. Minimum RAM, VRAM and full installed storage are not documented.
Software
uv Python toolchain; an MCP-capable LLM client, or Anthropic API for the standalone agent; downloadable LDraw parts.
Platforms
macOS is author-tested, Linux is documented. Windows via WSL is suggested but explicitly untested.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT server code; LDraw data and your LLM client/provider are separate dependencies.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT server code; LDraw data and your LLM client/provider are separate dependencies.
  • Cost: Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

Limitations

Organic shapes, curves and non-stud connection techniques are outside the shipped connection model. Software geometry reports do not prove real clutch strength or a safe physical structure. [1]

  2. Complete software license reviewed

How it uses AI

An agent skill and MCP server use vision to identify photographed parts, keep an inventory and help an LLM design constrained brick models with rendered build instructions. [1]

Introduction

An agent skill and MCP server use vision to identify photographed parts, keep an inventory and help an LLM design constrained brick models with rendered build instructions. [1]

What it is good for

Making a small concept from parts you already own and checking the proposed inventory before producing PNG or vertical/horizontal MP4 instructions. [1]

Demo & examples

The README links a short author demo and a tagged hackathon submission. Part recognition and assembly checks are documented, not independently validated by this review. [1]

Install

Use the official supported agent plugin/MCP setup. Local use needs Node 20+; the first server launch installs dependencies and Chromium. FFmpeg is needed only for MP4. The hosted alternative requires its OAuth sign-in and stores data with its own services. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the official supported agent plugin/MCP setup. Local use needs Node 20+; the first server launch installs dependencies and Chromium. FFmpeg is needed only for MP4. The hosted alternative requires its OAuth sign-in and stores data with its own services.
  3. The final command is the documented Claude Code route. Choose scripts/install.sh desktop for a printed local MCP configuration instead; use only one route.
git clone https://github.com/elcodabra/lego-build-inventory.git
cd lego-build-inventory
scripts/install.sh claude-code

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Follow the README connector instructions for your chosen agent; choose local or hosted handling deliberately.
  2. Photograph separated parts on a plain background and correct the agent’s inventory before requesting a model.
  3. Start with a supported template, review missing parts and connection checks, then render instructions and test the small assembly.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1]

Hardware
The README estimates about 550 MB on disk for first-run Chromium. Minimum RAM/VRAM and total storage are not documented.
Software
Node 20+, a supported agent/MCP host; FFmpeg for MP4; first-run Chromium download. Brickognize recognition needs network access.
Platforms
Agent/MCP integrations are documented for desktop and hosted web clients; a tested per-OS matrix is not documented.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1]

  • Weights: MIT project code; model-provider accounts, Brickognize service, hosted OAuth/storage and rendering dependencies retain separate terms. The optional model_analyze_photo route sends a reduced photograph to the Meta Model API; its access, contributor-mode and billing terms are separate.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT project code; model-provider accounts, Brickognize service, hosted OAuth/storage and rendering dependencies retain separate terms.
  • Cost: Local runtime plus your chosen agent/model plan. Hosted storage, tunnels and external recognition may have separate limits and costs.

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1]

A pile photographed at once is interpreted approximately by the chat model. How it uses AI

A multilingual neural text-to-speech and voice-cloning runtime offers native CUDA, MLX, C++/ggml and in-browser WebGPU backends with a shared voice-studio API. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Introduction

A multilingual neural text-to-speech and voice-cloning runtime offers native CUDA, MLX, C++/ggml and in-browser WebGPU backends with a shared voice-studio API. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

What it is good for

Creating a consented voice-over, auditioning lines in several languages and exporting speech for a film, interactive character or installation. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Demo & examples

The author supplies a browser-local playground, model card and evaluation pages. The backends expose different capabilities: the browser does not run Python ASR/plugin workers, so a successful server example does not establish identical browser features. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Install

For a no-server audition use the official WebGPU playground in a compatible browser. For Apple Silicon, obtain the recursive source, run uv sync for zonos3-mlx, fetch the GGUF companions and use the MLX launcher. Linux CUDA and native ggml have separate build and model instructions; do not mix old companions with a current backbone. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. For a no-server audition use the official WebGPU playground in a compatible browser. For Apple Silicon, obtain the recursive source, run uv sync for zonos3-mlx, fetch the GGUF companions and use the MLX launcher. Linux CUDA and native ggml have separate build and model instructions; do not mix old companions with a current backbone.
git clone --recurse-submodules https://github.com/Zyphra/ZONOS3-Mini.git
cd ZONOS3-Mini
uv sync --locked --package zonos3-mlx
hf download Zyphra/ZONOS3-Mini --include "gguf/*" --local-dir servers/ggml
servers/mlx/scripts/serve.sh

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  1. Choose a backend whose documented capabilities cover the intended speech task.
  2. Open the local studio at 127.0.0.1:1919, select a default voice or your consented reference and generate a short passage.
  3. Listen for pronunciation, language drift, clipping and voice consistency; export WAV and review the result in your editor before longer narration.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM/VRAM is not documented for all backends. The ggml guide states that downloading the entire optional gguf folder is about 9.3 GB; that is not the minimum download or runtime memory.
Software
Python 3.12 and uv for Python servers; Apple MLX on Apple Silicon; Linux NVIDIA/CUDA for the SGL server; CMake/Rust/submodules for ggml; WebGPU for browser use. Read the chosen backend guide.
Platforms
Linux NVIDIA, Apple Silicon MLX, native CPU/Metal/CUDA/Vulkan ggml and WebGPU browser routes are documented separately. This is not proof of every OS/device/backend combination.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  • Weights: MIT runtime code; the current official Hugging Face card declares Apache-2.0 model weights. The attempted root LICENSE file on the weight repository returned 404; retain that full-text weight-documentation gap. NOTICE lists separate third-party software.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT runtime code; the current official Hugging Face card declares Apache-2.0 model weights. The attempted root LICENSE file on the weight repository returned 404; retain that full-text weight-documentation gap. NOTICE lists separate third-party software.
  • Cost: Local compute/model downloads for local backends; the optional hosted Zyphra Cloud service has separate access and pricing.

How it uses AI

An Apple MLX media studio runs local video/image/speech/music models and combines shots in an editing timeline. The software is open source; downloaded model permissions vary substantially. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Introduction

An Apple MLX media studio runs local video/image/speech/music models and combines shots in an editing timeline. The software is open source; downloaded model permissions vary substantially. [1] [3] [4] [5]

What it is good for

Auditioning local text-to-video or image-to-video shots on an Apple Silicon Mac, then revising a sequence and sound in the editor. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Demo & examples

The current repository and primary changelog show version 5.0.17 on November 11. The author’s clips and timing examples are documentation evidence, not an independent benchmark or a guarantee for a smaller Mac. The newer 5.0.18 release, published during this review, reports 113 s versus 152 s for one 5-second LTX-2.5 clip on an M4 Max/64 GB. This is an author benchmark for the optional Pro pack, not independently verified performance or the free route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

Choose the official native Mac app or install the repository through Pinokio’s Download from URL flow. Setup itself downloads no models; choose an eligible model in the application after checking its exact terms and disk requirement. Follow the official manual for source installation. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Choose the official native Mac app or install the repository through Pinokio’s Download from URL flow. Setup itself downloads no models; choose an eligible model in the application after checking its exact terms and disk requirement. Follow the official manual for source installation.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Install the official Mac app or use the documented Pinokio source URL.
  2. Choose a model permitted for your location and use, start at a small supported resolution and inspect picture, audio and lip timing.
  3. Keep useful takes, revise the timeline and export a short test; review model terms before commercial output.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Hardware
The author documents smaller LTX video from 16 GB unified memory, higher modes at larger memory tiers, and about 27.5 GB for LTX Q4 weights. Setup needs 18 GB free including a 15 GB reserve; optional H3 is about 75 GB and Music Studio about 11 GB. These are author figures, not universal limits. The optional 5.0.18 Pro speed-up is enabled at 32 GB+ and downloads a separate 1.2 GB add-on; these figures do not replace the base workflow requirements.
Software
Apple Silicon/MLX. Native app and Pinokio installation are documented; the manual contains the source route. A universal minimum macOS version was not established in this review.
Platforms
Apple Silicon M1–M5 Macs. Intel Macs and Windows are explicitly unsupported. A separate Apple-Silicon Linux port is best-effort, not a general Linux support guarantee.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5]

  • Weights: MIT application code. LTX-2.5 and H3 use separate community terms; the README explicitly excludes EU, UK, South Korea and US from H3’s license. Several optional image/music models are non-commercial; open downloads do not make those weights open source. The optional Pro speed-up pack has separate distribution/access conditions not established by this root-code review.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT application code. LTX-2.5 and H3 use separate community terms; the README explicitly excludes EU, UK, South Korea and US from H3’s license. Several optional image/music models are non-commercial; open downloads do not make those weights open source.
  • Cost: Local compute/storage; optional Pro early-access/speed features and external model conditions are separate. The newly documented speed-up requires Pro access; no current subscription amount is established here.

cd overdub
node server/serve.js

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Software
WebGPU-capable browser for use. Source builds use Rust, wasm32-unknown-unknown and pinned Trunk 0.21.14, plus the documented build tools.
Platforms
The author tested Chrome on macOS with specific Intel/AMD GPUs. Other named WebGPU browsers/devices are suggested but explicitly not all tested.

How it uses AI

A 2D skeletal-animation editor exposes its editing operations over MCP so an external AI agent can rig or animate the same document a human edits; runtimes carry the result into games or interactive pages. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A 2D skeletal-animation editor exposes its editing operations over MCP so an external AI agent can rig or animate the same document a human edits; runtimes carry the result into games or interactive pages. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Creating a small rigged character with interactive state changes, then exporting a portable animation or embedding it in a target engine. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The author supplies Walker/Windswept examples and a live gallery. Their artwork, voices and sounds include separately licensed material; a shipped example does not grant blanket reuse or prove an agent will animate your rig well. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Use the official desktop build for the selected OS or clone the source and run pnpm install then pnpm dev. The source editor opens at localhost:5180. The MCP package is a separate documented agent entry point; choose only the runtime adapter matching your destination engine. [1] [3] [4]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Open a small example or import art you may use, then inspect the skeleton and animation tracks.
  2. Register the documented @openbone/mcp server in your client and request one reversible rig/animation change.
  3. Review motion and state behavior, save a new .obone and test its target-runtime export before a complete character.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Node/pnpm for the web editor; Rust/Tauri dependencies for desktop source builds. Runtime versions are engine-specific (for example Unity 2021.3+ or Godot 4.4+). A separate AI client/model is needed for agent editing.
Platforms
Official installers are documented for Windows, macOS (Apple Silicon/Intel) and Linux; web and multiple engine runtimes have separate compatibility requirements.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
The README recommends 8 GB+ VRAM for its general Windows GPU route and 16 GB+ unified memory on Mac; it also lists a CPU profile with 8 GB RAM. Profile downloads are about 5–13 GB; engine size differs (roughly 925 MB Windows CUDA, under 40 MB other listed builds). These are author profile figures, not universal minima.
Software
Windows CUDA/Vulkan, Apple Metal or Linux Vulkan engine as documented; compatible GGUF/safetensors models, text encoder and VAE. Internet is needed for first downloads. Source running uses Python 3.12+.
Platforms
Windows 10/11 is author-tested; macOS 13+ Apple Silicon is author-tested on M3; Linux x64 with Vulkan is explicitly experimental. Intel macOS support is not documented.

  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT application. The README identifies Apache-2.0 models in its advised FLUX.2-klein/Qwen profile; alternate weights and engines retain their own licenses and must be checked separately.
  • Cost: Local compute and storage; no paid generation API is required for the documented local route.

Install the PyPI package for a release-matched guide, run northcel doctor and guide, and check which renderer is selected. Native execution remains Linux x86-64/spec 0.3; newer documents use the labelled Python reference or optional Vello. Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. The documentation mixes release and unreleased spec examples. A reference-renderer result is not native-runtime performance evidence, and reproducible bytes do not prove that the drawing matches the brief. The new 0.7.1 release explicitly changes documentation only: no rendering semantics changed, the native runtime stays 0.4.0 and northcel-vello stays 0.7.0. Do not treat its version bump as a new rendering capability.

How it uses AI

A drawing language and CLI provide an AI agent with editable contour/layer programs and repeatable frame rendering. It is a dedicated agent-authoring workflow, not an image-generation model. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A drawing language and CLI provide an AI agent with editable contour/layer programs and repeatable frame rendering. It is a dedicated agent-authoring workflow, not an image-generation model. [1] [3] [4]

Making code-drawn animated illustrations where an agent can change one shape or timing region and re-render the affected frames. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Native execution remains Linux x86-64/spec 0.3; newer documents use the labelled Python reference or optional Vello. The npm package delegates full validation/rendering to a Python install rather than being a standalone renderer. [1] [3] [4]

First project

  3. Render a still and a short sequence, inspect the renderer receipt and output, and reject poor in-betweens rather than treating conformance hashes as aesthetic evidence.

Software
Python >=3.10 according to the current PyPI 0.7.1 record. Native execution is Linux x86-64/spec 0.3; newer documents use the labelled Python reference or optional Vello. Source-only/perception extras have their own dependencies.
Platforms
Native Linux x86-64 requires glibc 2.39+ in the current documentation. macOS, Windows and other Linux architectures use the Python reference; they do not inherit native GPU performance.

Install

Use Docker Desktop and the official setup guide/installer, or configure the source Compose setup. Set the allowed media folder and choose local Ollama, hosted Gemini or no AI. Generate local session/encryption secrets and map the media folder before docker compose up. [1]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use Docker Desktop and the official setup guide/installer, or configure the source Compose setup. Set the allowed media folder and choose local Ollama, hosted Gemini or no AI. Generate local session/encryption secrets and map the media folder before docker compose up.
git clone https://github.com/IliasHad/edit-mind.git
cd edit-mind
docker compose up

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1]

  1. Complete the README configuration before starting Compose; choose which local media folder containers may read.
  2. Open localhost:3745 (127.0.0.1 for the documented Safari route), create an account, add the mapped folder and rescan a small sample.
  3. Try several known-answer searches, compare returned scenes against the originals and inspect transcription/indexing errors before adding a large archive.

Docker Desktop and macOS/Linux terminal or Windows Git Bash/WSL setup routes are documented. GPU/model backend compatibility remains route-specific.

How it uses AI

A specialized AI coding harness drives a persistent headless Blender session with bpy scripts and staged validation, so an LLM can inspect and revise generated 3D assets. [1] [3]

Introduction

A specialized AI coding harness drives a persistent headless Blender session with bpy scripts and staged validation, so an LLM can inspect and revise generated 3D assets. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Supervised procedural asset generation in Blender with a record of scene changes and validation results. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The README describes staged checks and the Blender session contract. A validation gate can catch some API/geometry errors but cannot establish aesthetic quality or a production-ready asset. [1] [3]

Install

The runtime harness pins Blender 5.2. Either review the official platform installer/release route or build from source using Bun. In a source checkout, install dependencies, set BLENDER_PATH to your Blender executable, bootstrap Blender Python dependencies, build libfive for your actual platform, and register the launcher. Run axiom3d from the output project folder. Windows instructions require a real Bash environment such as Git Bash, WSL or MSYS2. [1] [3]

  1. Install the pinned Blender 5.2 release and choose a documented binary or source route.
  2. For a source build, use Bun and a compatible native build toolchain; replace the Blender path and platform placeholder with your own values.
  3. Open the asset-output project directory and start axiom3d. Review generated geometry and exported files.
git clone https://github.com/VossGloves/Axiom3D.git
cd Axiom3D
bun install
export BLENDER_PATH="/path/to/blender"
blender --background --factory-startup --python blender/bootstrap.py
./blender/build-libfive.sh <linux-x64|linux-arm64|darwin-x64|darwin-arm64|windows-x64>
bun link

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Install the documented pinned Blender/toolchain and start with a disposable asset project.
  2. Ask for a simple mesh, inspect the staged checks and rendered views, then make a small revision.
  3. Review topology, materials and scale in Blender and test export in the intended target engine.

Linux/macOS and Windows via WSL, Git Bash or MSYS2 setup routes are documented. They are not evidence that every Blender/backend combination was tested.

The repository was created on 2026-11-06. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-11 · 2 GitHub stars · Release v0.1.0 (2026-11-06) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Making block-style animated shorts with explicit camera/character directions and reusable code scenes.

Evidence checked 2026-11-11; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The README provides a film gallery and rendered examples. No video foundation model is built in; the AI role is the external assistant authoring the film code. [1] [3]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Obtain the canonical repository (the old trailing-hyphen name redirects). Use the supported agent project/skill workflow or official Claude Code plugin, then follow its local rendering dependencies. Do not treat the Minecraft-style look as access to Mojang assets. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT code/docs; original bundled textures/skins are CC0 according to the repository. Minecraft trademarks and any imported game assets remain separately controlled. Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate. [1] [3]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. This is code-generated animation, not a Minecraft game renderer or an open video model. Independent reproduction and complete system requirements remain unverified.

How it uses AI

An agent skill guides a coding assistant to produce editable Minecraft-style storyboards, characters and Three.js scenes, then renders them with a generated score into MP4. [1] [3]

Introduction

An agent skill guides a coding assistant to produce editable Minecraft-style storyboards, characters and Three.js scenes, then renders them with a generated score into MP4. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Making block-style animated shorts with explicit camera/character directions and reusable code scenes. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The README provides a film gallery and rendered examples. No video foundation model is built in; the AI role is the external assistant authoring the film code. [1] [3]

Install

Obtain the canonical repository (the old trailing-hyphen name redirects). Use the supported agent project/skill workflow or official Claude Code plugin, then follow its local rendering dependencies. Do not treat the Minecraft-style look as access to Mojang assets. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Obtain the canonical repository (the old trailing-hyphen name redirects). Use the supported agent project/skill workflow or official Claude Code plugin, then follow its local rendering dependencies. Do not treat the Minecraft-style look as access to Mojang assets.
  3. Install Node.js 20+, Python 3.11+ and FFmpeg/ffprobe. The documented local renderer uses npm ci and Chromium; use the repository guide for the selected engine/project.
claude plugin marketplace add ViniciusNoetzold/mine-blockreel-skill
claude plugin install blockreel@blockreel-market

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Start in the cloned authoring project and select the documented film workflow in your supported agent.
  2. Ask for a brief original scene, review the storyboard and rendered contact sheet, and refine camera/timing.
  3. Render a short MP4 and check assets, sound and credits before sharing it.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Node.js 20+, Python 3.11+, FFmpeg and ffprobe on PATH, a supported coding agent and the supplied renderer dependencies installed with npm ci. Rendering uses Chromium and can use software graphics.
Platforms
The agent workflow is documented; a tested per-OS runtime matrix is not provided in the reviewed README.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Weights: MIT code/docs; original bundled textures/skins are CC0 according to the repository. Minecraft trademarks and any imported game assets remain separately controlled.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT code/docs; original bundled textures/skins are CC0 according to the repository. Minecraft trademarks and any imported game assets remain separately controlled.
  • Cost: Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3]

Limitations

This is code-generated animation, not a Minecraft game renderer or an open video model. Independent reproduction and complete system requirements remain unverified. [1] [3]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. OCR is not verification of factual content or faithful page layout. Handwriting, damaged scans and unusual scripts need source-specific checking; a browser UI alone does not prove every GPU/backend is supported.

How it uses AI

A browser/Node OCR package runs PP-OCRv6-Small through LiteRT.js, exposing a tesseract.js-style API and document/image output for text, Markdown and JSON. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A browser/Node OCR package runs PP-OCRv6-Small through LiteRT.js, exposing a tesseract.js-style API and document/image output for text, Markdown and JSON. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Recovering text from a scan, photographed label or screenshot for an accessible creative archive or editorial workflow. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The official browser demo shows line boxes and recognized text. The sample latency is an author example, not a cross-device benchmark; visual/editorial proofreading is still needed. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Install the npm package for application integration, or use the official browser demo for an audition. Follow the README createWorker API and its runtime/model configuration; keep the package’s NOTICE with redistributed builds. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Install the npm package for application integration, or use the official browser demo for an audition. Follow the README createWorker API and its runtime/model configuration; keep the package’s NOTICE with redistributed builds.
npm install litert-ocr

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Try a short owned scan in the browser demo or a minimal createWorker integration.
  2. Inspect detected line boxes and compare recognition, reading order and punctuation with the original.
  3. Export text/Markdown/JSON and manually correct layout-sensitive or low-contrast content.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Compatible browser or Node.js runtime, LiteRT.js and the selected model assets. Exact current browser/Node minimum versions were not established in this review.
Platforms
Browser and Node operation are documented, not a universal desktop/mobile support guarantee.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4]

  • Weights: Apache-2.0 application code; copied LiteRT sample code, runtime and model terms are retained in NOTICE and remain distinct from your document rights.
  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. Apache-2.0 application code; copied LiteRT sample code, runtime and model terms are retained in NOTICE and remain distinct from your document rights.
  • Cost: Local compute plus any chosen external model or service; provider access and charges are separate.

Three-Live / XR-Live

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · Apache-2.0 · first-profile

First complete guide in this library, including completion of any earlier screened entry; not a claim of a launch today.

Docs checked 2026-11-11 · 320 GitHub stars · Release v1.4.0 (2026-11-10) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Prototyping a procedural 3D object or spatial interaction once, then comparing its web, mobile and headset runtime routes.

Evidence checked 2026-11-11; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The official studios and current repository demonstrate scene editing and runtime adapters. The November 3 launch article is older than the current 1.4.0 source/release; its initial preview wording does not alone classify today’s code as Experimental. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: For web integration install three-live/xr-live through npm, or obtain a pinned release bundle. The CLI can be installed from the three-live-compiler crate or an OS-specific release binary. Native Android and Apple integrations have separate JitPack/AAR and SwiftPM/XCFramework instructions; choose one route rather than installing every toolchain. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: Apache-2.0 software. Three.js/engine dependencies, mobile OS SDKs, hosted endpoints and chosen model/texture permissions remain separate. No foundation-model weights are included. Local compile/render compute; any selected language/image/voice provider incurs its own model or service costs. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. This is not an officially supported Google product. Compiled-scene quotas, lints and author sandbox claims are not an independent security or quality audit. Static Scene Viewer/Quick Look handoff does not preserve every live interaction; browser WebXR and native AR are different routes. The changed default-branch documentation expands optional scientific geometry kits and texture controls. With an image endpoint configured, missing textures may generate automatically; ai-textures="manual" selects explicit generation. Pin a matching release/bundle and assess costs and interactions on that route.

How it uses AI

An AI scene-authoring toolkit compiles an external model’s procedural Three.js into portable 3D assets and interactive native/web scenes, with CLI/MCP inspection and image-to-scene/editing integrations. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

An AI scene-authoring toolkit compiles an external model’s procedural Three.js into portable 3D assets and interactive native/web scenes, with CLI/MCP inspection and image-to-scene/editing integrations. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Prototyping a procedural 3D object or spatial interaction once, then comparing its web, mobile and headset runtime routes. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The official studios and current repository demonstrate scene editing and runtime adapters. The November 3 launch article is older than the current 1.4.0 source/release; its initial preview wording does not alone classify today’s code as Experimental. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

For web integration install three-live/xr-live through npm, or obtain a pinned release bundle. The CLI can be installed from the three-live-compiler crate or an OS-specific release binary. Native Android and Apple integrations have separate JitPack/AAR and SwiftPM/XCFramework instructions; choose one route rather than installing every toolchain. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. For web integration install three-live/xr-live through npm, or obtain a pinned release bundle. The CLI can be installed from the three-live-compiler crate or an OS-specific release binary. Native Android and Apple integrations have separate JitPack/AAR and SwiftPM/XCFramework instructions; choose one route rather than installing every toolchain.
npm install three-live xr-live
cargo install three-live-compiler --bin three-live-cli

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Start from an official small scene and your chosen runtime route.
  2. Use the documented agent/MCP or configured AI endpoint for a narrow scene edit, and inspect the contact sheet and spatial diagnostics.
  3. Export GLB/USDZ or test the live scene on the target device; verify interactions separately from a static AR handoff.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
WebGL2/WebXR-capable browser for the web route; Rust/Cargo for a source CLI install, or a matching prebuilt CLI. Native Android/Apple routes use their own SDK/engine toolchains. AI endpoints/models are separately configured.
Platforms
CLI binaries are documented for macOS arm64/x86-64, Linux x86-64/aarch64 and Windows x86-64. Native Android/iOS/macOS/visionOS and browser routes have distinct feature/device limits.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  • Weights: Apache-2.0 software. Three.js/engine dependencies, mobile OS SDKs, hosted endpoints and chosen model/texture permissions remain separate. No foundation-model weights are included.
  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. Apache-2.0 software. Three.js/engine dependencies, mobile OS SDKs, hosted endpoints and chosen model/texture permissions remain separate. No foundation-model weights are included.
  • Cost: Local compile/render compute; any selected language/image/voice provider incurs its own model or service costs.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Limitations

This is not an officially supported Google product. Compiled-scene quotas, lints and author sandbox claims are not an independent security or quality audit. Static Scene Viewer/Quick Look handoff does not preserve every live interaction; browser WebXR and native AR are different routes. The changed default-branch documentation expands optional scientific geometry kits and texture controls. With an image endpoint configured, missing textures may generate automatically; ai-textures="manual" selects explicit generation. Pin a matching release/bundle and assess costs and interactions on that route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

How it uses AI

A live generative-visual GUI combines camera/video/shader/mask inputs with neural image models and exposes live prompt and parameter controls for performance. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A live generative-visual GUI combines camera/video/shader/mask inputs with neural image models and exposes live prompt and parameter controls for performance. [1] [3] [4]

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Choose a compatible NVIDIA system and verify the selected model’s license and engine requirements.
  2. Install through the documented official route, build/load the engine, and begin with a simple camera/video source.
  3. Adjust prompt and layer parameters, inspect frame rate/latency and rehearse the exact performance route before using it live.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Hardware
The README specifies NVIDIA RTX 2000-generation or newer, 8 GB VRAM for SD1.5 or 12 GB for SDXL. The docs say 20 GB free RAM if training. Total disk and a universal generation RAM minimum are not documented.
Software
NVIDIA CUDA GPU and supported drivers; TensorRT engine preparation and separately obtained model. Linux requires official NVIDIA drivers, not Nouveau.
Platforms
Windows and Linux. macOS support is not documented for this CUDA workflow.

How it uses AI

A live-video computer-vision GUI loads ONNX pose/classification models and sends inference results over OSC to creative applications and custom interactive systems. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Introduction

A live-video computer-vision GUI loads ONNX pose/classification models and sends inference results over OSC to creative applications and custom interactive systems. [1] [3] [4] [5]

What it is good for

Mapping body movement into sound, visuals or installation parameters in an OSC-capable tool such as Pure Data, Processing or TouchDesigner. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Demo & examples

The official quick start documents model loading, camera selection and OSC output. The source README names BlazePose, YOLOv8 Pose and ResNet examples, not a guarantee that arbitrary ONNX inputs/outputs are compatible. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Install

Use SAT’s platform download/setup route and obtain an appropriate separately licensed ONNX model from the documented storage/source. Ready-to-install Creator builds currently require an account/subscription; review the public source/build route independently. Connect a camera and configure the destination OSC receiver. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use SAT’s platform download/setup route and obtain an appropriate separately licensed ONNX model from the documented storage/source. Ready-to-install Creator builds currently require an account/subscription; review the public source/build route independently. Connect a camera and configure the destination OSC receiver.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5]

  1. Select a supported model and verify its expected camera/input shape and output mapping.
  2. Launch LivePose, select the camera, load the ONNX model and configure OSC host/port.
  3. Watch the received data in a small creative patch, calibrate scale/confidence and test occlusion and tracking loss before a live audience.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
A supported desktop platform, camera, compatible ONNX model and OSC receiver. GPU/NPU acceleration, if selected, has separate backend/device requirements.
Platforms
macOS, Linux x86-64 and Windows are documented. Minimum OS versions and a complete accelerator support matrix were not established.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5]

  • Weights: The complete root license combines GPLv3 software terms with retained third-party attribution and a CeCILL provenance notice explicitly treating the whole distribution as GPLv3. ONNX is a format, not a license for every model.
  • Commercial: Software use follows GPL-3.0. The complete root license combines GPLv3 software terms with retained third-party attribution and a CeCILL provenance notice explicitly treating the whole distribution as GPLv3. ONNX is a format, not a license for every model.
  • Cost: Local inference/camera compute; SAT ready-to-install Creator builds have current account/subscription conditions. Optional proprietary creative hosts and models have separate terms.

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Complete code-license and creative-AI source review; author demonstrations do not establish independent quality or all dependency rights.

Scope: Offline instruction-guided editing of short source videos on Apple Silicon using the released checkpoints.

Evidence checked 2026-11-11; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: Read the complete Apache-2.0 software text; operative sections 1-9 match the retained standard with no additional purpose/commercial restriction. Preserve notices. Model checkpoints and upstream dependencies retain separate terms and require their own review. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: Author video edits and parity/performance measurements are described, not independently assessed. [1]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Clone, uv, checkpoint and conversion instructions are present; independent reproduction and quantized-memory verification remain gaps. [1]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers were not independently established.
  • Independent use · unknown: No independent first-hand production use was established for this exact MLX editing route.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: JoyAI and MiMo checkpoints are separate large downloads; complete weight permissions were not established. [1]

Documentation review only; no discovered software was installed or run. Quantized memory claims need route-specific verification; full model terms and independent first-hand use remain unresolved.

How it uses AI

A 16B video diffusion model and vision-language conditioning edit a source clip from a natural-language prompt, including local changes and scene restyling. [1] [2]

Introduction

An Apple MLX port of JoyAI-Video-Edit runs instruction-guided source-video edits with a diffusion transformer, causal video VAE and MiMo-VL conditioning. It is an offline/near-line editor on the documented Mac hardware. [1] [2]

What it is good for

Testing prompt-guided restyles, local changes, background edits and reference-image-conditioned clip edits on an adequately provisioned Apple Silicon machine. [1] [2]

Demo & examples

The author reports numerical/parity checks and real 480p edits on an M3 Ultra. Its measured roughly 0.9 FPS is an author result, not upstream real-time GPU speed or our own benchmark. [1] [2]

Install

Clone the official GitLab source, create the uv environment, download JoyAI and MiMo checkpoints from their upstreams and convert them once using the documented joyai_edit.convert command. The model files are not bundled. Read the raw/checkpoint and converted-path instructions before the large download. [1] [2]

  1. Install macOS 14+, Python 3.12+ and uv on Apple Silicon.
  2. Clone the official repository and run uv sync.
  3. Follow the README checkpoint-download and conversion steps, keeping source and converted folders explicit.
git clone https://gitlab.com/celestialtech/joyai-video-edit-mlx.git
cd joyai-video-edit-mlx
uv sync

First project

Use a short source clip and the converted weights to test a narrow edit before a long batch. [1] [2]

  1. Run the documented editor CLI with input video, a short instruction, output path and --weights folder.
  2. Alternatively start uv run joyai-edit-server --port 8765 and use its local video/webcam UI.
  3. Compare requested edits, identity/temporal consistency and sound handling with the source, then review the exported frames.

Hardware & software

Keep author measurements, planned/quantized routes and tested baseline distinct. [1] [2]

Hardware
The README reports roughly 57 GB for the full bf16 working set (64 GB comfortable, 96 GB+ preferred) and about 120 GB disk for raw plus converted weights. Its q4/32 GB descriptions are inconsistent between the requirements list and later performance section; treat that route as needing verification, not a proven minimum.
Software
macOS 14+, Python 3.12+, uv and MLX 0.31+ in the documented environment. Source model downloads and conversion are required. Development/parity dependencies are separate.
Platforms
Apple Silicon macOS. This MLX port does not establish Windows, Intel Mac or Linux support.

License, model weights & costs

Full software grant reviewed; model rights remain separate. [1] [2]

  • Weights: JoyAI-Video-Edit and MiMo-VL checkpoints are supplied by their respective upstreams. Their complete weight terms were not established in this review.
  • Commercial: Apache-2.0 applies to this port’s code. Do not infer commercial model/output permissions from that code grant.
  • Cost: Local Mac compute and substantial model storage. No paid inference API is required for the documented local route.

Why it merits attention

Included for a concrete, documented local AI editing workflow. Author parity checks are not independent creator-use or output-quality evidence. [1] [2]

Limitations

This is not the upstream 24 FPS datacenter-GPU workflow. Performance, memory, prompt adherence and quantized behavior must be checked against the exact local hardware and material; model permissions and independent reproduction remain gaps. [1] [2]

ClipForge — Whisper captions and audio mix

Documented creative workflow; quality confidence assessed separately · MIT · recently-created

The repository was created on 2026-11-02. This is its first full guide in this library, not independent validation of a new release.

Docs checked 2026-11-11 · 0 GitHub stars · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Making a simple captioned MP4 from an owned video and a separate voice track, with the caption style editable in source.

Evidence checked 2026-11-11; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code; unresolved model, service and asset terms are disclosed separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The README includes a workflow illustration and specifies output.mp4. The inspected app.py establishes the actual Whisper/MoviePy path, not independently verified transcription or a benchmark-quality demo. [1] [3] [4]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Clone the source, create and activate a Python virtual environment, install the two unpinned requirements, and install FFmpeg separately on PATH. Place video.mp4 and voice.mp3 in the project directory. Windows activation uses .venv\Scripts\activate; macOS/Linux use source .venv/bin/activate. The first Whisper run may download its model. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT script code. Whisper model/runtime permissions and MoviePy/FFmpeg/font/input-media terms remain separate; the repository does not bundle every dependency. Local compute and model download; this documented inference path does not call a paid transcription API. [1] [3] [4]

Documentation review only; AI Media Scout did not install or run this software. One video and one extra track per execution; filenames, model and caption styling are fixed in the script. No GUI, independent reproduction or output-quality evaluation is established. Missing inputs, silent source video and unpinned dependency compatibility are not resolved by the README.

How it uses AI

A local Python script uses the Whisper base neural speech model to derive segment timestamps, then burns captions onto real footage and mixes an additional voice track with MoviePy. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

A local Python script uses the Whisper base neural speech model to derive segment timestamps, then burns captions onto real footage and mixes an additional voice track with MoviePy. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Making a simple captioned MP4 from an owned video and a separate voice track, with the caption style editable in source. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The README includes a workflow illustration and specifies output.mp4. The inspected app.py establishes the actual Whisper/MoviePy path, not independently verified transcription or a benchmark-quality demo. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Clone the source, create and activate a Python virtual environment, install the two unpinned requirements, and install FFmpeg separately on PATH. Place video.mp4 and voice.mp3 in the project directory. Windows activation uses .venv\Scripts\activate; macOS/Linux use source .venv/bin/activate. The first Whisper run may download its model. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Clone the source, create and activate a Python virtual environment, install the two unpinned requirements, and install FFmpeg separately on PATH. Place video.mp4 and voice.mp3 in the project directory. Windows activation uses .venv\Scripts\activate; macOS/Linux use source .venv/bin/activate. The first Whisper run may download its model.
git clone https://github.com/abenezerwassefa-byte/ClipForge.git
cd ClipForge
python -m venv .venv
pip install -r requirements.txt
python app.py

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Activate the new environment before installing requirements; choose a small owned clip with a spoken audio track.
  2. Place video.mp4 and voice.mp3 beside app.py. The current source hardcodes these names, uses Whisper base, sets fp16=False and scales the extra voice track to 0.2.
  3. Run python app.py, inspect the burned captions, segment timing and audio balance in output.mp4, and edit the source style/settings if needed. Keep originals.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
Python, MoviePy, openai-whisper and FFmpeg. Minimum Python/package versions are not pinned in the current requirements file; model and font/runtime compatibility need checking.
Platforms
The README documents Windows and Linux/macOS environment activation. A tested OS/version/device matrix is not established.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4]

  • Weights: MIT script code. Whisper model/runtime permissions and MoviePy/FFmpeg/font/input-media terms remain separate; the repository does not bundle every dependency.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT script code. Whisper model/runtime permissions and MoviePy/FFmpeg/font/input-media terms remain separate; the repository does not bundle every dependency.
  • Cost: Local compute and model download; this documented inference path does not call a paid transcription API.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3] [4]

Limitations

One video and one extra track per execution; filenames, model and caption styling are fixed in the script. No GUI, independent reproduction or output-quality evaluation is established. Missing inputs, silent source video and unpinned dependency compatibility are not resolved by the README. [1] [3] [4]
